[
  {
    "role": "system",
    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n15\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF50.9, F52.9\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well… I came in because I can’t keep pretending I’m fine after these nights when I eat like that. It’s not just the food, exactly. It’s afterward. I’ll be sitting there with crumbs on me, empty containers around me, and this horrible feeling just comes over me—shame, disgust with myself, like I’ve done something I can’t even explain.\n\nAnd the next morning it follows me. I’m a churchgoing woman, and I’ll try to pray, but I can hardly get the words out because I feel so ashamed. I don’t even want to look people in the eye at church, like they can somehow see what I did the night before. It’s gotten so heavy that I thought… I need to talk to somebody, because I don’t know how to carry it by myself anymore.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have control over eating that way on those nights?\n\nPatient: No… no, that’s the awful part. I feel like I should have control, because I’m the one sitting there, I’m the one picking it up, you know? But once I take that first bite after the house is quiet, it’s like something in me just… steps aside. My good sense, my better judgment, whatever you want to call it, it just moves out of the way.\n\nAnd I’ll be saying to myself the whole time, “Belinda, stop now. That’s enough. Put it away. You don’t need any more.” I can hear myself thinking it, clear as day. But my hands just keep reaching. I’ll tell myself I’m done, and then I’m opening something else, or going back for more, like my body isn’t listening to me. It scares me, honestly, because I don’t feel like I’m choosing it the way I ought to be able to choose.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you lose control in other areas of your life, besides eating?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… I mean, not in that same way, I don’t think. I’m trying to think honestly because I don’t want to say the wrong thing. With the eating, it feels very clear to me, like there’s that moment where I know I should stop and I just don’t.\n\nIn other parts of my life… I can get overwhelmed, sure. I can worry too much or put things off, and sometimes I feel like my thoughts run around in circles. But I don’t know if I’d call that losing control. Not like this. I haven’t noticed myself doing things in other areas where I feel taken over the same way, if that makes sense.\n\nMaybe if you mean something specific, I could answer better. But what I’m coming in for—the thing that feels out of my hands—is really the eating.\n\nDoctor: Do you usually feel guilty after these episodes of eating?\n\nPatient: Yes… very guilty. Guilty almost isn’t a strong enough word sometimes. After it happens, I’ll look around and see the containers and wrappers, crumbs on my clothes or on the chair, and I just feel this wave of shame come over me. Like, “What is wrong with me? Why did I do that again?”\n\nAnd it doesn’t just go away when I clean it up. The next morning it’s still sitting on me. I’m a churchgoing woman, and I’ll try to pray, but sometimes I can hardly get the words out because I feel so ashamed and disgusted with myself. I’ll be around folks at church and not want to look them in the eye, even though they don’t know anything about it. It feels like they could somehow see it on me. So yes… afterward, the guilt is terrible.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had episodes of eating like that where you didn't feel guilty afterward?\n\nPatient: No… I can’t really think of a time where I didn’t feel guilty afterward. That’s a big part of why I came in, honestly. It’s not just the eating itself, though that’s bad enough. It’s what comes after.\n\nI’ll be sitting there afterward, and there’ll be crumbs on me, empty containers around me, and I just feel so ashamed and disgusted with myself. Like I’ve done something terrible, even if nobody else saw it. And the next morning it’s still with me. I’ll try to pray, and sometimes I can hardly even do that because I feel so low about myself. Or I’ll go to church and I don’t want to look folks in the eye, even though they don’t know anything about what happened.\n\nSo no, I don’t remember feeling fine afterward. It’s always that shame, that guilt, like I’ve let myself down again.\n\nDoctor: Do you usually feel hungry before these episodes of eating happen?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure that I am, not most of the time. I mean, sometimes I might be a little hungry, like anyone would be if it’s been a while since I ate, but that doesn’t seem to be what starts it, if that makes sense.\n\nA lot of times I don’t stop and check, “Am I hungry?” It’s more like I get pulled toward the food, and then once I start it just keeps going. I’ve tried to think afterward, “Was I even hungry?” and honestly, sometimes I don’t think I was. Or at least not hungry enough to explain all of that.\n\nSo… I don’t know. I wouldn’t say it usually happens because I’m hungry. It feels different from regular hunger, but I’m not always good at noticing what I’m feeling right before it starts.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're eating quickly during these episodes?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, I do. Very quickly. It’s not like sitting down to a meal and enjoying it. A lot of times I’m standing right there at the kitchen counter, and I’ll be eating pound cake or crackers so fast I hardly even taste them.\n\nSometimes I’ll realize I’m swallowing before I’ve properly chewed, like my body is just rushing ahead of me. And then I’ll think, “Slow down, what are you doing?” but even then it’s hard to stop or even slow it. It feels hurried, almost frantic, and afterward I can barely remember enjoying any of it.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had an episode of eating like this where you ate a large amount of food in a short time, but didn't feel guilty or ashamed afterward?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think so. I know I’ve said it already, but that guilty feeling is almost always right there after. That’s actually a big part of why I’m here.\n\nAfter one of those nights, I’ll look around and see crumbs on me, empty containers sitting there, and I just feel… ashamed. Disgusted with myself, really. It’s not like I can just say, “Well, that happened,” and move on. It sits on me. Sometimes I feel so low afterward I can hardly even pray, and the next morning if I go to church, I don’t want to look folks in the eye. They don’t know what I did, but I still feel like they can somehow see it on me.\n\nSo, no… I can’t remember eating that way and then feeling all right about it afterward. It’s the shame afterward that makes it so hard.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever eaten a large amount of food in a short time without feeling guilty or ashamed afterward?\n\nPatient: No… no, I really don’t think I have. I know I may be repeating myself, but that’s honestly why I came in. It’s not just the eating itself, it’s what comes after.\n\nAfter those nights, I’ll be sitting there and there are crumbs on me, empty containers around me, and it’s like everything goes quiet and I just feel this awful shame. Disgusted with myself, really. I’ll think, “Why did I do that again?” And it’s not a small feeling, either. Sometimes I feel so low afterward I can hardly even pray.\n\nAnd the next morning, if I go to church, I don’t even want to look folks in the eye. They don’t know what happened, of course, but I feel like they can see it on me somehow. So no, I can’t really remember a time where I ate like that and then didn’t feel guilty or ashamed afterward.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're in a trance or on autopilot when you're eating during these episodes?\n\nPatient: Yes… that’s a good way to put it, I think. Not exactly like I’m unconscious or anything, but once the house is quiet and I take that first bite, it’s like something in me just… shifts. My good sense steps aside.\n\nI can be standing there knowing full well I should stop. I’ll even be saying it in my head, sometimes almost like I’m scolding myself: “Belinda, stop now. Put it away. You don’t need any more.” But my hands just keep moving, reaching for more like they’re not listening to me. I’ll open another package or cut another piece, and part of me is watching it happen, thinking, “Why are you still doing this?”\n\nSo yes, it does feel like autopilot in that way. I’m there, I know what’s happening, but I don’t feel like I’m the one steering.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had an episode where you ate a large amount of food in a short time and didn't feel guilty or ashamed about it afterward, but still felt like you lost control while eating?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, that part is true. I know I keep saying the guilt is there afterward, because it is, but during it, it’s the losing control that scares me.\n\nIt usually starts once the house is quiet. I’ll take that first bite, and it’s like my good sense just steps aside. I don’t mean I don’t know what I’m doing — I do know. I’m aware enough to be thinking, Belinda, stop now. Put it away. You’ve had enough. But even while I’m saying that to myself, my hands keep going back for more. Reaching, opening things, taking another bite, then another.\n\nSo no, I don’t really have those times where I eat that much and feel fine about it afterward. But yes, while it’s happening, I do feel like I’ve lost control. It’s like I’m watching myself keep going when I already know I want to stop.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel a sense of pleasure or euphoria while you're eating during these episodes?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d call it pleasure, exactly… and euphoria sounds too strong to me, like being happy or lifted up, and I don’t think that’s what it feels like.\n\nMaybe there’s a moment right at the beginning where it feels like relief, if that makes sense. Like everything else gets pushed out of my mind for a minute and I’m just focused on the food. But it’s not joyful. I’m not sitting there feeling wonderful or excited in a good way. It’s more like… I don’t know… a kind of numbness, or a pull. Like I’m trying to soothe something, but I wouldn’t say I’m enjoying it the way a person enjoys a nice meal.\n\nSo no, I haven’t really noticed euphoria. If there is any good feeling, it’s very short and it gets swallowed up pretty quickly by that sense of, “What am I doing?”\n\nDoctor: Have you ever eaten a large amount of food in a short time without feeling guilty or ashamed afterward, even if you felt like you lost control while eating?\n\nPatient: No… no, I don’t think I’ve had it where I ate like that and then didn’t feel guilty or ashamed. That’s really the hard part for me afterward.\n\nI mean, the reason I finally came in is because after those nights, when it’s over, I’ll be sitting there and there are crumbs on me or on the table, empty containers around, wrappers… and I just feel so ashamed. Disgusted with myself, really. It’s like the quiet comes back and I can see everything I did, and I think, “How did you let this happen again?”\n\nAnd then the next morning it follows me. I have trouble even praying sometimes because I feel so low about myself, and if I go to church I feel like I can hardly look people in the eye. I know they may not know anything, but I feel like it’s written all over me somehow.\n\nSo yes, I lose control while I’m eating, but afterward… no, I don’t feel fine about it. I feel terrible.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever eaten a large amount of food in a very short time and not felt guilty about it?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think so. Disgusted with myself, if I’m being honest.\n\nAnd it doesn’t just end when I clean it up. The next morning it’s still sitting on me. The guilt is usually right there afterward.\n\nDoctor: Do you experience any physical symptoms, such as stomach pain or nausea, after these episodes of eating?\n\nPatient: Yes… I do. That part is very noticeable afterward.\n\nI’ll eat past the point where I know I should stop, and then I keep going anyway, and by the time it’s over my stomach feels tight, like stretched tight and sore. So no, I can’t really remember a time where I ate like that and then didn’t feel guilty or ashamed afterward.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're in a trance or on autopilot when you're eating during these episodes?\n\nPatient: Yes… that’s a good way to put it, I think. Not exactly like I’m unconscious or anything, but once the house is quiet and I take that first bite, it’s like something in me just… shifts. My good sense steps aside.\n\nI can be standing there knowing full well I should stop. I’ll even be saying it in my head, sometimes almost like I’m scolding myself: “Belinda, stop now. Put it away. You don’t need any more.” But my hands just keep moving, reaching for more like they’re not listening to me. I’ll open another package or cut another piece, and part of me is watching it happen, thinking, “Why are you still doing this?”\n\nSo yes, it does feel like autopilot in that way. I’m there, I know what’s happening, but I don’t feel like I’m the one steering.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had an episode where you ate a large amount of food in a short time and didn't feel guilty or ashamed about it afterward, but still felt like you lost control while eating?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, that part is true. I know I keep saying the guilt is there afterward, because it is, but during it, it’s the losing control that scares me.\n\nIt usually starts once the house is quiet. I’ll take that first bite, and it’s like my good sense just steps aside. I don’t mean I don’t know what I’m doing — I do know. I’m aware enough to be thinking, Belinda, stop now. Put it away. You’ve had enough. But even while I’m saying that to myself, my hands keep going back for more. Reaching, opening things, taking another bite, then another.\n\nSo no, I don’t really have those times where I eat that much and feel fine about it afterward. But yes, while it’s happening, I do feel like I’ve lost control. It’s like I’m watching myself keep going when I already know I want to stop.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel a sense of pleasure or euphoria while you're eating during these episodes?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d call it pleasure, exactly… and euphoria sounds too strong to me, like being happy or lifted up, and I don’t think that’s what it feels like.\n\nMaybe there’s a moment right at the beginning where it feels like relief, if that makes sense. Like everything else gets pushed out of my mind for a minute and I’m just focused on the food. But it’s not joyful. I’m not sitting there feeling wonderful or excited in a good way. It’s more like… I don’t know… a kind of numbness, or a pull. Like I’m trying to soothe something, but I wouldn’t say I’m enjoying it the way a person enjoys a nice meal.\n\nSo no, I haven’t really noticed euphoria. If there is any good feeling, it’s very short and it gets swallowed up pretty quickly by that sense of, “What am I doing?”\n\nDoctor: Have you ever eaten a large amount of food in a short time without feeling guilty or ashamed afterward, even if you felt like you lost control while eating?\n\nPatient: No… no, I don’t think I’ve had it where I ate like that and then didn’t feel guilty or ashamed. That’s really the hard part for me afterward.\n\nI mean, the reason I finally came in is because after those nights, when it’s over, I’ll be sitting there and there are crumbs on me or on the table, empty containers around, wrappers… and I just feel so ashamed. Disgusted with myself, really. It’s like the quiet comes back and I can see everything I did, and I think, “How did you let this happen again?”\n\nAnd then the next morning it follows me. I have trouble even praying sometimes because I feel so low about myself, and if I go to church I feel like I can hardly look people in the eye. I know they may not know anything, but I feel like it’s written all over me somehow.\n\nSo yes, I lose control while I’m eating, but afterward… no, I don’t feel fine about it. I feel terrible.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever eaten a large amount of food in a very short time and not felt guilty about it?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think so. Not the way you mean.\n\nWhen it’s one of those times where I’ve eaten a lot in a short stretch, afterward I don’t feel calm or like it was nothing. That’s actually a big part of why I came in. I’ll sort of come back to myself and be sitting there with crumbs on me, or on the chair, empty containers and wrappers around, and I just feel… ashamed. Really ashamed. Disgusted with myself, if I’m being honest.\n\nAnd it doesn’t just end when I clean it up. The next morning it’s still sitting on me. Sometimes I can hardly pray because I feel so low, and if I go to church I feel like I can’t look folks in the eye, even though they don’t know what happened. I just feel like they can somehow see it on me.\n\nSo no, I can’t say I’ve eaten like that and not felt guilty. The guilt is usually right there afterward.\n\nDoctor: Do you experience any physical symptoms, such as stomach pain or nausea, after these episodes of eating?\n\nPatient: Yes… I do. That part is very noticeable afterward.\n\nI’ll eat past the point where I know I should stop, and then I keep going anyway, and by the time it’s over my stomach feels tight, like stretched tight and sore. Sometimes I can feel it pressing under my nightgown, and it’s uncomfortable even just sitting there. It’s not just “oh, I’m full from supper” kind of full. It’s more like I’ve made myself so full that my body is complaining.\n\nAnd moving around afterward hurts. I’ve had times where I sort of have to ease myself into the recliner slowly, because if I bend too much or move too fast, my stomach feels sore and heavy. I’ll sit there trying to get comfortable, but there isn’t really a comfortable way to sit for a while. It’s embarrassing to say, but yes, I definitely get physical discomfort after.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. No… not really. I know I’ve kind of said this, but that’s the part that stays with me the most. When I eat like that, afterward I don’t feel relieved or okay about it. I sit there and look around at the crumbs, the empty containers, the wrappers, and I just feel so ashamed of myself.

Sometimes I can hardly pray because I feel so low, and if I go to church I feel like I can’t look people in the eye, even though they don’t know. I just feel like somehow it’s written all over me.

What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well… I came in because I can’t keep pretending I’m fine after these nights when I eat like that. It’s not just the food, exactly. It’s afterward. I’ll be sitting there with crumbs on me, empty containers around me, and this horrible feeling just comes over me—shame, disgust with myself, like I’ve done something I can’t even explain.\n\nAnd the next morning it follows me. I don’t even want to look people in the eye at church, like they can somehow see what I did the night before. It’s gotten so heavy that I thought… I need to talk to somebody, because I don’t know how to carry it by myself anymore.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have control over eating that way on those nights?\n\nPatient: No… no, that’s the awful part. I feel like I should have control, because I’m the one sitting there, I’m the one picking it up, you know? But once I take that first bite after the house is quiet, it’s like something in me just… steps aside. My good sense, my better judgment, whatever you want to call it, it just moves out of the way.\n\nAnd I’ll be saying to myself the whole time, “Belinda, stop now. That’s enough. Put it away. You don’t need any more.” I can hear myself thinking it, clear as day. But my hands just keep reaching. I’ll tell myself I’m done, and then I’m opening something else, or going back for more, like my body isn’t listening to me. It scares me, honestly, because I don’t feel like I’m choosing it the way I ought to be able to choose.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you lose control in other areas of your life, besides eating?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… I mean, not in that same way, I don’t think. I’m trying to think honestly because I don’t want to say the wrong thing. With the eating, it feels very clear to me, like there’s that moment where I know I should stop and I just don’t.\n\nIn other parts of my life… I can get overwhelmed, sure. I can worry too much or put things off, and sometimes I feel like my thoughts run around in circles. But I don’t know if I’d call that losing control. Not like this. I haven’t noticed myself doing things in other areas where I feel taken over the same way, if that makes sense.\n\nMaybe if you mean something specific, I could answer better. But what I’m coming in for—the thing that feels out of my hands—is really the eating.\n\nDoctor: Do you usually feel guilty after these episodes of eating?\n\nPatient: Yes… very guilty. Guilty almost isn’t a strong enough word sometimes. After it happens, I’ll look around and see the containers and wrappers, crumbs on my clothes or on the chair, and I just feel this wave of shame come over me. Like, “What is wrong with me? Why did I do that again?”\n\nAnd it doesn’t just go away when I clean it up. The next morning it’s still sitting on me. I’m a churchgoing woman, and I’ll try to pray, but sometimes I can hardly get the words out because I feel so ashamed and disgusted with myself. I’ll be around folks at church and not want to look them in the eye, even though they don’t know anything about it. It feels like they could somehow see it on me. So yes… afterward, the guilt is terrible.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had episodes of eating like that where you didn't feel guilty afterward?\n\nPatient: No… I can’t really think of a time where I didn’t feel guilty afterward. That’s a big part of why I came in, honestly. It’s not just the eating itself, though that’s bad enough. It’s what comes after.\n\nI’ll be sitting there afterward, and there’ll be crumbs on me, empty containers around me, and I just feel so ashamed and disgusted with myself. Like I’ve done something terrible, even if nobody else saw it. And the next morning it’s still with me. I’ll try to pray, and sometimes I can hardly even do that because I feel so low about myself. Or I’ll go to church and I don’t want to look folks in the eye, even though they don’t know anything about what happened.\n\nSo no, I don’t remember feeling fine afterward. It’s always that shame, that guilt, like I’ve let myself down again.\n\nDoctor: Do you usually feel hungry before these episodes of eating happen?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure that I am, not most of the time. I mean, sometimes I might be a little hungry, like anyone would be if it’s been a while since I ate, but that doesn’t seem to be what starts it, if that makes sense.\n\nA lot of times I don’t stop and check, “Am I hungry?” It’s more like I get pulled toward the food, and then once I start it just keeps going. I’ve tried to think afterward, “Was I even hungry?” and honestly, sometimes I don’t think I was. Or at least not hungry enough to explain all of that.\n\nSo… I don’t know. I wouldn’t say it usually happens because I’m hungry. It feels different from regular hunger, but I’m not always good at noticing what I’m feeling right before it starts.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're eating quickly during these episodes?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, I do. Very quickly. It’s not like sitting down to a meal and enjoying it. A lot of times I’m standing right there at the kitchen counter, and I’ll be eating pound cake or crackers so fast I hardly even taste them.\n\nSometimes I’ll realize I’m swallowing before I’ve properly chewed, like my body is just rushing ahead of me. And then I’ll think, “Slow down, what are you doing?” but even then it’s hard to stop or even slow it. It feels hurried, almost frantic, and afterward I can barely remember enjoying any of it.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had an episode of eating like this where you ate a large amount of food in a short time, but didn't feel guilty or ashamed afterward?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think so. I know I’ve said it already, but that guilty feeling is almost always right there after. That’s actually a big part of why I’m here.\n\nAfter one of those nights, I’ll look around and see crumbs on me, empty containers sitting there, and I just feel… ashamed. Disgusted with myself, really. It’s not like I can just say, “Well, that happened,” and move on. It sits on me. Sometimes I feel so low afterward I can hardly even pray, and the next morning if I go to church, I don’t want to look folks in the eye. They don’t know what I did, but I still feel like they can somehow see it on me.\n\nSo, no… I can’t remember eating that way and then feeling all right about it afterward. It’s the shame afterward that makes it so hard.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever eaten a large amount of food in a short time without feeling guilty or ashamed afterward?\n\nPatient: No… no, I really don’t think I have. I know I may be repeating myself, but that’s honestly why I came in. It’s not just the eating itself, it’s what comes after.\n\nAfter those nights, I’ll be sitting there and there are crumbs on me, empty containers around me, and it’s like everything goes quiet and I just feel this awful shame. Disgusted with myself, really. I’ll think, “Why did I do that again?” And it’s not a small feeling, either. Sometimes I feel so low afterward I can hardly even pray.\n\nAnd the next morning, if I go to church, I don’t even want to look folks in the eye. They don’t know what happened, of course, but I feel like they can see it on me somehow. So no, I can’t really remember a time where I ate like that and then didn’t feel guilty or ashamed afterward.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're in a trance or on autopilot when you're eating during these episodes?\n\nPatient: Yes… that’s a good way to put it, I think. Not exactly like I’m unconscious or anything, but once the house is quiet and I take that first bite, it’s like something in me just… shifts. My good sense steps aside.\n\nI can be standing there knowing full well I should stop. I’ll even be saying it in my head, sometimes almost like I’m scolding myself: “Belinda, stop now. Put it away. You don’t need any more.” But my hands just keep moving, reaching for more like they’re not listening to me. I’ll open another package or cut another piece, and part of me is watching it happen, thinking, “Why are you still doing this?”\n\nSo yes, it does feel like autopilot in that way. I’m there, I know what’s happening, but I don’t feel like I’m the one steering.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had an episode where you ate a large amount of food in a short time and didn't feel guilty or ashamed about it afterward, but still felt like you lost control while eating?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, that part is true. I know I keep saying the guilt is there afterward, because it is, but during it, it’s the losing control that scares me.\n\nIt usually starts once the house is quiet. I’ll take that first bite, and it’s like my good sense just steps aside. I don’t mean I don’t know what I’m doing — I do know. I’m aware enough to be thinking, Belinda, stop now. Put it away. You’ve had enough. But even while I’m saying that to myself, my hands keep going back for more. Reaching, opening things, taking another bite, then another.\n\nSo no, I don’t really have those times where I eat that much and feel fine about it afterward. But yes, while it’s happening, I do feel like I’ve lost control. It’s like I’m watching myself keep going when I already know I want to stop.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel a sense of pleasure or euphoria while you're eating during these episodes?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d call it pleasure, exactly… and euphoria sounds too strong to me, like being happy or lifted up, and I don’t think that’s what it feels like.\n\nMaybe there’s a moment right at the beginning where it feels like relief, if that makes sense. Like everything else gets pushed out of my mind for a minute and I’m just focused on the food. But it’s not joyful. I’m not sitting there feeling wonderful or excited in a good way. It’s more like… I don’t know… a kind of numbness, or a pull. Like I’m trying to soothe something, but I wouldn’t say I’m enjoying it the way a person enjoys a nice meal.\n\nSo no, I haven’t really noticed euphoria. If there is any good feeling, it’s very short and it gets swallowed up pretty quickly by that sense of, “What am I doing?”\n\nDoctor: Have you ever eaten a large amount of food in a short time without feeling guilty or ashamed afterward, even if you felt like you lost control while eating?\n\nPatient: No… no, I don’t think I’ve had it where I ate like that and then didn’t feel guilty or ashamed. That’s really the hard part for me afterward.\n\nI mean, the reason I finally came in is because after those nights, when it’s over, I’ll be sitting there and there are crumbs on me or on the table, empty containers around, wrappers… and I just feel so ashamed. Disgusted with myself, really. It’s like the quiet comes back and I can see everything I did, and I think, “How did you let this happen again?”\n\nAnd then the next morning it follows me. I have trouble even praying sometimes because I feel so low about myself, and if I go to church I feel like I can hardly look people in the eye. I know they may not know anything, but I feel like it’s written all over me somehow.\n\nSo yes, I lose control while I’m eating, but afterward… no, I don’t feel fine about it. I feel terrible.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever eaten a large amount of food in a very short time and not felt guilty about it?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think so. Not the way you mean.\n\nWhen it’s one of those times where I’ve eaten a lot in a short stretch, afterward I don’t feel calm or like it was nothing. That’s actually a big part of why I came in. I’ll sort of come back to myself and be sitting there with crumbs on me, or on the chair, empty containers and wrappers around, and I just feel… ashamed. Really ashamed. Disgusted with myself, if I’m being honest.\n\nAnd it doesn’t just end when I clean it up. The next morning it’s still sitting on me. Sometimes I can hardly pray because I feel so low, and if I go to church I feel like I can’t look folks in the eye, even though they don’t know what happened. I just feel like they can somehow see it on me.\n\nSo no, I can’t say I’ve eaten like that and not felt guilty. The guilt is usually right there afterward.\n\nDoctor: Do you experience any physical symptoms, such as stomach pain or nausea, after these episodes of eating?\n\nPatient: Yes… I do. That part is very noticeable afterward.\n\nI’ll eat past the point where I know I should stop, and then I keep going anyway, and by the time it’s over my stomach feels tight, like stretched tight and sore. Sometimes I can feel it pressing under my nightgown, and it’s uncomfortable even just sitting there. It’s not just “oh, I’m full from supper” kind of full. It’s more like I’ve made myself so full that my body is complaining.\n\nAnd moving around afterward hurts. I’ve had times where I sort of have to ease myself into the recliner slowly, because if I bend too much or move too fast, my stomach feels sore and heavy. I’ll sit there trying to get comfortable, but there isn’t really a comfortable way to sit for a while. It’s embarrassing to say, but yes, I definitely get physical discomfort after.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever eaten a large amount of food in a short time without feeling guilty or ashamed about it afterward?\n\nPatient: No… not really. I know I’ve kind of said this, but that’s the part that stays with me the most. When I eat like that, afterward I don’t feel relieved or okay about it. I sit there and look around at the crumbs, the empty containers, the wrappers, and I just feel so ashamed of myself.\n\nIt’s not just a little guilt, either. I feel disgusted with myself, like I’ve done something I should’ve been able to stop. And the next morning it’s still there. Sometimes I can hardly pray because I feel so low, and if I go to church I feel like I can’t look people in the eye, even though they don’t know. I just feel like somehow it’s written all over me.\n\nSo no, I don’t think I’ve had those times without guilt or shame. The shame is usually right there afterward.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. That part is very noticeable afterward.\n\nI’ll eat past the point where I know I should stop, and then I keep going anyway, and by the time it’s over my stomach feels tight, like stretched tight and sore. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yes… yes, that’s exactly what it feels like. Not every bite of food in my life, of course, but when it happens, it’s like once I start—especially after the house is quiet—something in me just… steps aside.

I’ll take that first bite, and part of me already knows, Belinda, this is where it starts, you need to stop now. I can hear myself thinking it. I’m not confused about what I’m doing. But then my hands just keep reaching for more anyway, like my good sense is standing off in the corner and not in charge anymore.

And I’ll tell myself, okay, that’s enough, put it away, just close the package, just get up and leave the room. But I don’t. I keep eating, even while I’m arguing with myself in my own head. That’s the part that scares me, honestly—the feeling that I’m watching myself do it and I can’t seem to make myself stop when I want to.

[
  {
    "role": "system",
    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n17\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF50.9, F52.9\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well… I came in because I can’t keep pretending I’m fine after these nights when I eat like that. It’s not just the food, exactly. It’s afterward. I’ll be sitting there with crumbs on me, empty containers around me, and this horrible feeling just comes over me—shame, disgust with myself, like I’ve done something I can’t even explain.\n\nAnd the next morning it follows me. I’m a churchgoing woman, and I’ll try to pray, but I can hardly get the words out because I feel so ashamed. I don’t even want to look people in the eye at church, like they can somehow see what I did the night before. It’s gotten so heavy that I thought… I need to talk to somebody, because I don’t know how to carry it by myself anymore.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have control over eating that way on those nights?\n\nPatient: No… no, that’s the awful part. I feel like I should have control, because I’m the one sitting there, I’m the one picking it up, you know? But once I take that first bite after the house is quiet, it’s like something in me just… steps aside. My good sense, my better judgment, whatever you want to call it, it just moves out of the way.\n\nAnd I’ll be saying to myself the whole time, “Belinda, stop now. That’s enough. Put it away. You don’t need any more.” I can hear myself thinking it, clear as day. But my hands just keep reaching. I’ll tell myself I’m done, and then I’m opening something else, or going back for more, like my body isn’t listening to me. It scares me, honestly, because I don’t feel like I’m choosing it the way I ought to be able to choose.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you lose control in other areas of your life, besides eating?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… I mean, not in that same way, I don’t think. I’m trying to think honestly because I don’t want to say the wrong thing. With the eating, it feels very clear to me, like there’s that moment where I know I should stop and I just don’t.\n\nIn other parts of my life… I can get overwhelmed, sure. I can worry too much or put things off, and sometimes I feel like my thoughts run around in circles. But I don’t know if I’d call that losing control. Not like this. I haven’t noticed myself doing things in other areas where I feel taken over the same way, if that makes sense.\n\nMaybe if you mean something specific, I could answer better. But what I’m coming in for—the thing that feels out of my hands—is really the eating.\n\nDoctor: Do you usually feel guilty after these episodes of eating?\n\nPatient: Yes… very guilty. Guilty almost isn’t a strong enough word sometimes. After it happens, I’ll look around and see the containers and wrappers, crumbs on my clothes or on the chair, and I just feel this wave of shame come over me. Like, “What is wrong with me? Why did I do that again?”\n\nAnd it doesn’t just go away when I clean it up. The next morning it’s still sitting on me. I’m a churchgoing woman, and I’ll try to pray, but sometimes I can hardly get the words out because I feel so ashamed and disgusted with myself. I’ll be around folks at church and not want to look them in the eye, even though they don’t know anything about it. It feels like they could somehow see it on me. So yes… afterward, the guilt is terrible.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had episodes of eating like that where you didn't feel guilty afterward?\n\nPatient: No… I can’t really think of a time where I didn’t feel guilty afterward. That’s a big part of why I came in, honestly. It’s not just the eating itself, though that’s bad enough. It’s what comes after.\n\nI’ll be sitting there afterward, and there’ll be crumbs on me, empty containers around me, and I just feel so ashamed and disgusted with myself. Like I’ve done something terrible, even if nobody else saw it. And the next morning it’s still with me. I’ll try to pray, and sometimes I can hardly even do that because I feel so low about myself. Or I’ll go to church and I don’t want to look folks in the eye, even though they don’t know anything about what happened.\n\nSo no, I don’t remember feeling fine afterward. It’s always that shame, that guilt, like I’ve let myself down again.\n\nDoctor: Do you usually feel hungry before these episodes of eating happen?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure that I am, not most of the time. I mean, sometimes I might be a little hungry, like anyone would be if it’s been a while since I ate, but that doesn’t seem to be what starts it, if that makes sense.\n\nA lot of times I don’t stop and check, “Am I hungry?” It’s more like I get pulled toward the food, and then once I start it just keeps going. I’ve tried to think afterward, “Was I even hungry?” and honestly, sometimes I don’t think I was. Or at least not hungry enough to explain all of that.\n\nSo… I don’t know. I wouldn’t say it usually happens because I’m hungry. It feels different from regular hunger, but I’m not always good at noticing what I’m feeling right before it starts.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're eating quickly during these episodes?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, I do. Very quickly. It’s not like sitting down to a meal and enjoying it. A lot of times I’m standing right there at the kitchen counter, and I’ll be eating pound cake or crackers so fast I hardly even taste them.\n\nSometimes I’ll realize I’m swallowing before I’ve properly chewed, like my body is just rushing ahead of me. And then I’ll think, “Slow down, what are you doing?” but even then it’s hard to stop or even slow it. It feels hurried, almost frantic, and afterward I can barely remember enjoying any of it.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had an episode of eating like this where you ate a large amount of food in a short time, but didn't feel guilty or ashamed afterward?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think so. I know I’ve said it already, but that guilty feeling is almost always right there after. That’s actually a big part of why I’m here.\n\nAfter one of those nights, I’ll look around and see crumbs on me, empty containers sitting there, and I just feel… ashamed. Disgusted with myself, really. It’s not like I can just say, “Well, that happened,” and move on. It sits on me. Sometimes I feel so low afterward I can hardly even pray, and the next morning if I go to church, I don’t want to look folks in the eye. They don’t know what I did, but I still feel like they can somehow see it on me.\n\nSo, no… I can’t remember eating that way and then feeling all right about it afterward. It’s the shame afterward that makes it so hard.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever eaten a large amount of food in a short time without feeling guilty or ashamed afterward?\n\nPatient: No… no, I really don’t think I have. I know I may be repeating myself, but that’s honestly why I came in. It’s not just the eating itself, it’s what comes after.\n\nAfter those nights, I’ll be sitting there and there are crumbs on me, empty containers around me, and it’s like everything goes quiet and I just feel this awful shame. Disgusted with myself, really. I’ll think, “Why did I do that again?” And it’s not a small feeling, either. Sometimes I feel so low afterward I can hardly even pray.\n\nAnd the next morning, if I go to church, I don’t even want to look folks in the eye. They don’t know what happened, of course, but I feel like they can see it on me somehow. So no, I can’t really remember a time where I ate like that and then didn’t feel guilty or ashamed afterward.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're in a trance or on autopilot when you're eating during these episodes?\n\nPatient: Yes… that’s a good way to put it, I think. Not exactly like I’m unconscious or anything, but once the house is quiet and I take that first bite, it’s like something in me just… shifts. My good sense steps aside.\n\nI can be standing there knowing full well I should stop. I’ll even be saying it in my head, sometimes almost like I’m scolding myself: “Belinda, stop now. Put it away. You don’t need any more.” But my hands just keep moving, reaching for more like they’re not listening to me. I’ll open another package or cut another piece, and part of me is watching it happen, thinking, “Why are you still doing this?”\n\nSo yes, it does feel like autopilot in that way. I’m there, I know what’s happening, but I don’t feel like I’m the one steering.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had an episode where you ate a large amount of food in a short time and didn't feel guilty or ashamed about it afterward, but still felt like you lost control while eating?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, that part is true. I know I keep saying the guilt is there afterward, because it is, but during it, it’s the losing control that scares me.\n\nIt usually starts once the house is quiet. I’ll take that first bite, and it’s like my good sense just steps aside. I don’t mean I don’t know what I’m doing — I do know. I’m aware enough to be thinking, Belinda, stop now. Put it away. You’ve had enough. But even while I’m saying that to myself, my hands keep going back for more. Reaching, opening things, taking another bite, then another.\n\nSo no, I don’t really have those times where I eat that much and feel fine about it afterward. But yes, while it’s happening, I do feel like I’ve lost control. It’s like I’m watching myself keep going when I already know I want to stop.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel a sense of pleasure or euphoria while you're eating during these episodes?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d call it pleasure, exactly… and euphoria sounds too strong to me, like being happy or lifted up, and I don’t think that’s what it feels like.\n\nMaybe there’s a moment right at the beginning where it feels like relief, if that makes sense. Like everything else gets pushed out of my mind for a minute and I’m just focused on the food. But it’s not joyful. I’m not sitting there feeling wonderful or excited in a good way. It’s more like… I don’t know… a kind of numbness, or a pull. Like I’m trying to soothe something, but I wouldn’t say I’m enjoying it the way a person enjoys a nice meal.\n\nSo no, I haven’t really noticed euphoria. If there is any good feeling, it’s very short and it gets swallowed up pretty quickly by that sense of, “What am I doing?”\n\nDoctor: Have you ever eaten a large amount of food in a short time without feeling guilty or ashamed afterward, even if you felt like you lost control while eating?\n\nPatient: No… no, I don’t think I’ve had it where I ate like that and then didn’t feel guilty or ashamed. That’s really the hard part for me afterward.\n\nI mean, the reason I finally came in is because after those nights, when it’s over, I’ll be sitting there and there are crumbs on me or on the table, empty containers around, wrappers… and I just feel so ashamed. Disgusted with myself, really. It’s like the quiet comes back and I can see everything I did, and I think, “How did you let this happen again?”\n\nAnd then the next morning it follows me. I have trouble even praying sometimes because I feel so low about myself, and if I go to church I feel like I can hardly look people in the eye. I know they may not know anything, but I feel like it’s written all over me somehow.\n\nSo yes, I lose control while I’m eating, but afterward… no, I don’t feel fine about it. I feel terrible.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever eaten a large amount of food in a very short time and not felt guilty about it?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think so. Not the way you mean.\n\nWhen it’s one of those times where I’ve eaten a lot in a short stretch, afterward I don’t feel calm or like it was nothing. That’s actually a big part of why I came in. I’ll sort of come back to myself and be sitting there with crumbs on me, or on the chair, empty containers and wrappers around, and I just feel… ashamed. Really ashamed. Disgusted with myself, if I’m being honest.\n\nAnd it doesn’t just end when I clean it up. The next morning it’s still sitting on me. Sometimes I can hardly pray because I feel so low, and if I go to church I feel like I can’t look folks in the eye, even though they don’t know what happened. I just feel like they can somehow see it on me.\n\nSo no, I can’t say I’ve eaten like that and not felt guilty. The guilt is usually right there afterward.\n\nDoctor: Do you experience any physical symptoms, such as stomach pain or nausea, after these episodes of eating?\n\nPatient: Yes… I do. That part is very noticeable afterward.\n\nI’ll eat past the point where I know I should stop, and then I keep going anyway, and by the time it’s over my stomach feels tight, like stretched tight and sore. Sometimes I can feel it pressing under my nightgown, and it’s uncomfortable even just sitting there. It’s not just “oh, I’m full from supper” kind of full. It’s more like I’ve made myself so full that my body is complaining.\n\nAnd moving around afterward hurts. I’ve had times where I sort of have to ease myself into the recliner slowly, because if I bend too much or move too fast, my stomach feels sore and heavy. I’ll sit there trying to get comfortable, but there isn’t really a comfortable way to sit for a while. It’s embarrassing to say, but yes, I definitely get physical discomfort after.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever eaten a large amount of food in a short time without feeling guilty or ashamed about it afterward?\n\nPatient: No… not really. I know I’ve kind of said this, but that’s the part that stays with me the most. When I eat like that, afterward I don’t feel relieved or okay about it. I sit there and look around at the crumbs, the empty containers, the wrappers, and I just feel so ashamed of myself.\n\nIt’s not just a little guilt, either. The shame is usually right there afterward.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever eaten a large amount of food in a short time without feeling like you were in control while eating?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, that’s exactly what it feels like. Like I’ve done something terrible, even if nobody else saw it. And the next morning it’s still with me. I’ll try to pray, and sometimes I can hardly even do that because I feel so low about myself. Or I’ll go to church and I don’t want to look folks in the eye, even though they don’t know anything about what happened.\n\nSo no, I don’t remember feeling fine afterward. It’s always that shame, that guilt, like I’ve let myself down again.\n\nDoctor: Do you usually feel hungry before these episodes of eating happen?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure that I am, not most of the time. I mean, sometimes I might be a little hungry, like anyone would be if it’s been a while since I ate, but that doesn’t seem to be what starts it, if that makes sense.\n\nA lot of times I don’t stop and check, “Am I hungry?” It’s more like I get pulled toward the food, and then once I start it just keeps going. I’ve tried to think afterward, “Was I even hungry?” and honestly, sometimes I don’t think I was. Or at least not hungry enough to explain all of that.\n\nSo… I don’t know. I wouldn’t say it usually happens because I’m hungry. It feels different from regular hunger, but I’m not always good at noticing what I’m feeling right before it starts.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're eating quickly during these episodes?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, I do. Very quickly. It’s not like sitting down to a meal and enjoying it. A lot of times I’m standing right there at the kitchen counter, and I’ll be eating pound cake or crackers so fast I hardly even taste them.\n\nSometimes I’ll realize I’m swallowing before I’ve properly chewed, like my body is just rushing ahead of me. And then I’ll think, “Slow down, what are you doing?” but even then it’s hard to stop or even slow it. It feels hurried, almost frantic, and afterward I can barely remember enjoying any of it.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had an episode of eating like this where you ate a large amount of food in a short time, but didn't feel guilty or ashamed afterward?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think so. I know I’ve said it already, but that guilty feeling is almost always right there after. That’s actually a big part of why I’m here.\n\nAfter one of those nights, I’ll look around and see crumbs on me, empty containers sitting there, and I just feel… ashamed. Disgusted with myself, really. It’s not like I can just say, “Well, that happened,” and move on. It sits on me. Sometimes I feel so low afterward I can hardly even pray, and the next morning if I go to church, I don’t want to look folks in the eye. They don’t know what I did, but I still feel like they can somehow see it on me.\n\nSo, no… I can’t remember eating that way and then feeling all right about it afterward. It’s the shame afterward that makes it so hard.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever eaten a large amount of food in a short time without feeling guilty or ashamed afterward?\n\nPatient: No… no, I really don’t think I have. I know I may be repeating myself, but that’s honestly why I came in. It’s not just the eating itself, it’s what comes after.\n\nAfter those nights, I’ll be sitting there and there are crumbs on me, empty containers around me, and it’s like everything goes quiet and I just feel this awful shame. Disgusted with myself, really. I’ll think, “Why did I do that again?” And it’s not a small feeling, either. Sometimes I feel so low afterward I can hardly even pray.\n\nAnd the next morning, if I go to church, I don’t even want to look folks in the eye. They don’t know what happened, of course, but I feel like they can see it on me somehow. So no, I can’t really remember a time where I ate like that and then didn’t feel guilty or ashamed afterward.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're in a trance or on autopilot when you're eating during these episodes?\n\nPatient: Yes… that’s a good way to put it, I think. Not exactly like I’m unconscious or anything, but once the house is quiet and I take that first bite, it’s like something in me just… shifts. My good sense steps aside.\n\nI can be standing there knowing full well I should stop. I’ll even be saying it in my head, sometimes almost like I’m scolding myself: “Belinda, stop now. Put it away. You don’t need any more.” But my hands just keep moving, reaching for more like they’re not listening to me. I’ll open another package or cut another piece, and part of me is watching it happen, thinking, “Why are you still doing this?”\n\nSo yes, it does feel like autopilot in that way. I’m there, I know what’s happening, but I don’t feel like I’m the one steering.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had an episode where you ate a large amount of food in a short time and didn't feel guilty or ashamed about it afterward, but still felt like you lost control while eating?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, that part is true. I know I keep saying the guilt is there afterward, because it is, but during it, it’s the losing control that scares me.\n\nIt usually starts once the house is quiet. I’ll take that first bite, and it’s like my good sense just steps aside. I don’t mean I don’t know what I’m doing — I do know. I’m aware enough to be thinking, Belinda, stop now. Put it away. You’ve had enough. But even while I’m saying that to myself, my hands keep going back for more. Reaching, opening things, taking another bite, then another.\n\nSo no, I don’t really have those times where I eat that much and feel fine about it afterward. But yes, while it’s happening, I do feel like I’ve lost control. It’s like I’m watching myself keep going when I already know I want to stop.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel a sense of pleasure or euphoria while you're eating during these episodes?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d call it pleasure, exactly… and euphoria sounds too strong to me, like being happy or lifted up, and I don’t think that’s what it feels like.\n\nMaybe there’s a moment right at the beginning where it feels like relief, if that makes sense. Like everything else gets pushed out of my mind for a minute and I’m just focused on the food. But it’s not joyful. I’m not sitting there feeling wonderful or excited in a good way. It’s more like… I don’t know… a kind of numbness, or a pull. Like I’m trying to soothe something, but I wouldn’t say I’m enjoying it the way a person enjoys a nice meal.\n\nSo no, I haven’t really noticed euphoria. If there is any good feeling, it’s very short and it gets swallowed up pretty quickly by that sense of, “What am I doing?”\n\nDoctor: Have you ever eaten a large amount of food in a short time without feeling guilty or ashamed afterward, even if you felt like you lost control while eating?\n\nPatient: No… no, I don’t think I’ve had it where I ate like that and then didn’t feel guilty or ashamed. That’s really the hard part for me afterward.\n\nI mean, the reason I finally came in is because after those nights, when it’s over, I’ll be sitting there and there are crumbs on me or on the table, empty containers around, wrappers… and I just feel so ashamed. Disgusted with myself, really. It’s like the quiet comes back and I can see everything I did, and I think, “How did you let this happen again?”\n\nAnd then the next morning it follows me. I have trouble even praying sometimes because I feel so low about myself, and if I go to church I feel like I can hardly look people in the eye. I know they may not know anything, but I feel like it’s written all over me somehow.\n\nSo yes, I lose control while I’m eating, but afterward… no, I don’t feel fine about it. I feel terrible.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever eaten a large amount of food in a very short time and not felt guilty about it?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think so. Not the way you mean.\n\nWhen it’s one of those times where I’ve eaten a lot in a short stretch, afterward I don’t feel calm or like it was nothing. That’s actually a big part of why I came in. I’ll sort of come back to myself and be sitting there with crumbs on me, or on the chair, empty containers and wrappers around, and I just feel… ashamed. Really ashamed. Disgusted with myself, if I’m being honest.\n\nAnd it doesn’t just end when I clean it up. The next morning it’s still sitting on me. Sometimes I can hardly pray because I feel so low, and if I go to church I feel like I can’t look folks in the eye, even though they don’t know what happened. I just feel like they can somehow see it on me.\n\nSo no, I can’t say I’ve eaten like that and not felt guilty. The guilt is usually right there afterward.\n\nDoctor: Do you experience any physical symptoms, such as stomach pain or nausea, after these episodes of eating?\n\nPatient: Yes… I do. That part is very noticeable afterward.\n\nI’ll eat past the point where I know I should stop, and then I keep going anyway, and by the time it’s over my stomach feels tight, like stretched tight and sore. Sometimes I can feel it pressing under my nightgown, and it’s uncomfortable even just sitting there. It’s not just “oh, I’m full from supper” kind of full. It’s more like I’ve made myself so full that my body is complaining.\n\nAnd moving around afterward hurts. I’ve had times where I sort of have to ease myself into the recliner slowly, because if I bend too much or move too fast, my stomach feels sore and heavy. I’ll sit there trying to get comfortable, but there isn’t really a comfortable way to sit for a while. It’s embarrassing to say, but yes, I definitely get physical discomfort after.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever eaten a large amount of food in a short time without feeling guilty or ashamed about it afterward?\n\nPatient: No… not really. I know I’ve kind of said this, but that’s the part that stays with me the most. When I eat like that, afterward I don’t feel relieved or okay about it. I sit there and look around at the crumbs, the empty containers, the wrappers, and I just feel so ashamed of myself.\n\nIt’s not just a little guilt, either. I feel disgusted with myself, like I’ve done something I should’ve been able to stop. And the next morning it’s still there. Sometimes I can hardly pray because I feel so low, and if I go to church I feel like I can’t look people in the eye, even though they don’t know. I just feel like somehow it’s written all over me.\n\nSo no, I don’t think I’ve had those times without guilt or shame. The shame is usually right there afterward.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever eaten a large amount of food in a short time without feeling like you were in control while eating?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, that’s exactly what it feels like. I can hear myself thinking it. I’m not confused about what I’m doing. But then my hands just keep reaching for more anyway, like my good sense is standing off in the corner and not in charge anymore.\n\nAnd I’ll tell myself, okay, that’s enough, put it away, just close the package, just get up and leave the room. But I don’t. I keep eating, even while I’m arguing with myself in my own head. That’s the part that scares me, honestly—the feeling that I’m watching myself do it and I can’t seem to make myself stop when I want to.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
No… I can’t really think of a time like that. If I’ve lost control with eating, the shame is right there afterward. That’s honestly one of the main reasons I came in, because after those nights I’ll just sit there and see the crumbs and the empty containers around me, and I feel so ashamed and disgusted with myself.

It’s not like I finish and just move on with my evening. I sit there thinking, Why did I do this again? Why couldn’t I stop? And then the next morning it follows me. I can hardly pray sometimes because I feel so low about myself, and if I go to church I feel like I can’t look folks in the eye, even though nobody there knows what happened in my kitchen the night before.

So no, if I feel out of control while I’m eating, I don’t come away from it feeling fine. I feel awful about it afterward.

From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n18\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF50.9, F52.9\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well… I came in because I can’t keep pretending I’m fine after these nights when I eat like that. It’s not just the food, exactly. It’s afterward. I’ll be sitting there with crumbs on me, empty containers around me, and this horrible feeling just comes over me—shame, disgust with myself, like I’ve done something I can’t even explain.\n\nAnd the next morning it follows me. I’m a churchgoing woman, and I’ll try to pray, but I can hardly get the words out because I feel so ashamed. It’s gotten so heavy that I thought… I need to talk to somebody, because I don’t know how to carry it by myself anymore.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have control over eating that way on those nights?\n\nPatient: No… no, that’s the awful part. I feel like I should have control, because I’m the one sitting there, I’m the one picking it up, you know? You don’t need any more.” I can hear myself thinking it, clear as day. But my hands just keep reaching. I’ll tell myself I’m done, and then I’m opening something else, or going back for more, like my body isn’t listening to me. It scares me, honestly, because I don’t feel like I’m choosing it the way I ought to be able to choose.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you lose control in other areas of your life, besides eating?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… I mean, not in that same way, I don’t think. I’m trying to think honestly because I don’t want to say the wrong thing. With the eating, it feels very clear to me, like there’s that moment where I know I should stop and I just don’t.\n\nIn other parts of my life… I can get overwhelmed, sure. I can worry too much or put things off, and sometimes I feel like my thoughts run around in circles. But I don’t know if I’d call that losing control. Not like this. I haven’t noticed myself doing things in other areas where I feel taken over the same way, if that makes sense.\n\nMaybe if you mean something specific, I could answer better. But what I’m coming in for—the thing that feels out of my hands—is really the eating.\n\nDoctor: Do you usually feel guilty after these episodes of eating?\n\nPatient: Yes… very guilty. Guilty almost isn’t a strong enough word sometimes. After it happens, I’ll look around and see the containers and wrappers, crumbs on my clothes or on the chair, and I just feel this wave of shame come over me. Like, “What is wrong with me? Why did I do that again?”\n\nAnd it doesn’t just go away when I clean it up. The next morning it’s still sitting on me. I’m a churchgoing woman, and I’ll try to pray, but sometimes I can hardly get the words out because I feel so ashamed and disgusted with myself. I’ll be around folks at church and not want to look them in the eye, even though they don’t know anything about it. It feels like they could somehow see it on me. So yes… afterward, the guilt is terrible.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had episodes of eating like that where you didn't feel guilty afterward?\n\nPatient: No… I can’t really think of a time where I didn’t feel guilty afterward. That’s a big part of why I came in, honestly. It’s not just the eating itself, though that’s bad enough. It’s what comes after.\n\nI’ll be sitting there afterward, and there’ll be crumbs on me, empty containers around me, and I just feel so ashamed and disgusted with myself. Like I’ve done something terrible, even if nobody else saw it. And the next morning it’s still with me. I’ll try to pray, and sometimes I can hardly even do that because I feel so low about myself. Or I’ll go to church and I don’t want to look folks in the eye, even though they don’t know anything about what happened.\n\nSo no, I don’t remember feeling fine afterward. It’s always that shame, that guilt, like I’ve let myself down again.\n\nDoctor: Do you usually feel hungry before these episodes of eating happen?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure that I am, not most of the time. I mean, sometimes I might be a little hungry, like anyone would be if it’s been a while since I ate, but that doesn’t seem to be what starts it, if that makes sense.\n\nA lot of times I don’t stop and check, “Am I hungry?” It’s more like I get pulled toward the food, and then once I start it just keeps going. I’ve tried to think afterward, “Was I even hungry?” and honestly, sometimes I don’t think I was. Or at least not hungry enough to explain all of that.\n\nSo… I don’t know. I wouldn’t say it usually happens because I’m hungry. It feels different from regular hunger, but I’m not always good at noticing what I’m feeling right before it starts.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're eating quickly during these episodes?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, I do. Very quickly. It’s not like sitting down to a meal and enjoying it. A lot of times I’m standing right there at the kitchen counter, and I’ll be eating pound cake or crackers so fast I hardly even taste them.\n\nSometimes I’ll realize I’m swallowing before I’ve properly chewed, like my body is just rushing ahead of me. And then I’ll think, “Slow down, what are you doing?” but even then it’s hard to stop or even slow it. It feels hurried, almost frantic, and afterward I can barely remember enjoying any of it.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had an episode of eating like this where you ate a large amount of food in a short time, but didn't feel guilty or ashamed afterward?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think so. I know I’ve said it already, but that guilty feeling is almost always right there after. That’s actually a big part of why I’m here.\n\nAfter one of those nights, I’ll look around and see crumbs on me, empty containers sitting there, and I just feel… ashamed. Disgusted with myself, really. It’s not like I can just say, “Well, that happened,” and move on. It sits on me. Sometimes I feel so low afterward I can hardly even pray, and the next morning if I go to church, I don’t want to look folks in the eye. They don’t know what I did, but I still feel like they can somehow see it on me.\n\nSo, no… I can’t remember eating that way and then feeling all right about it afterward. It’s the shame afterward that makes it so hard.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever eaten a large amount of food in a short time without feeling guilty or ashamed afterward?\n\nPatient: No… no, I really don’t think I have. I know I may be repeating myself, but that’s honestly why I came in. It’s not just the eating itself, it’s what comes after.\n\nAfter those nights, I’ll be sitting there and there are crumbs on me, empty containers around me, and it’s like everything goes quiet and I just feel this awful shame. Disgusted with myself, really. I’ll think, “Why did I do that again?” And it’s not a small feeling, either. Sometimes I feel so low afterward I can hardly even pray.\n\nAnd the next morning, if I go to church, I don’t even want to look folks in the eye. They don’t know what happened, of course, but I feel like they can see it on me somehow. So no, I can’t really remember a time where I ate like that and then didn’t feel guilty or ashamed afterward.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're in a trance or on autopilot when you're eating during these episodes?\n\nPatient: Yes… that’s a good way to put it, I think. Not exactly like I’m unconscious or anything, but once the house is quiet and I take that first bite, it’s like something in me just… shifts. My good sense steps aside.\n\nI can be standing there knowing full well I should stop. I’ll even be saying it in my head, sometimes almost like I’m scolding myself: “Belinda, stop now. Put it away. You don’t need any more.” But my hands just keep moving, reaching for more like they’re not listening to me. I’ll open another package or cut another piece, and part of me is watching it happen, thinking, “Why are you still doing this?”\n\nSo yes, it does feel like autopilot in that way. I’m there, I know what’s happening, but I don’t feel like I’m the one steering.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had an episode where you ate a large amount of food in a short time and didn't feel guilty or ashamed about it afterward, but still felt like you lost control while eating?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, that part is true. I know I keep saying the guilt is there afterward, because it is, but during it, it’s the losing control that scares me.\n\nIt usually starts once the house is quiet. I’ll take that first bite, and it’s like my good sense just steps aside. I don’t mean I don’t know what I’m doing — I do know. I’m aware enough to be thinking, Belinda, stop now. Put it away. You’ve had enough. But even while I’m saying that to myself, my hands keep going back for more. Reaching, opening things, taking another bite, then another.\n\nSo no, I don’t really have those times where I eat that much and feel fine about it afterward. But yes, while it’s happening, I do feel like I’ve lost control. It’s like I’m watching myself keep going when I already know I want to stop.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel a sense of pleasure or euphoria while you're eating during these episodes?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d call it pleasure, exactly… and euphoria sounds too strong to me, like being happy or lifted up, and I don’t think that’s what it feels like.\n\nMaybe there’s a moment right at the beginning where it feels like relief, if that makes sense. Like everything else gets pushed out of my mind for a minute and I’m just focused on the food. But it’s not joyful. I’m not sitting there feeling wonderful or excited in a good way. It’s more like… I don’t know… a kind of numbness, or a pull. Like I’m trying to soothe something, but I wouldn’t say I’m enjoying it the way a person enjoys a nice meal.\n\nSo no, I haven’t really noticed euphoria. If there is any good feeling, it’s very short and it gets swallowed up pretty quickly by that sense of, “What am I doing?”\n\nDoctor: Have you ever eaten a large amount of food in a short time without feeling guilty or ashamed afterward, even if you felt like you lost control while eating?\n\nPatient: No… no, I don’t think I’ve had it where I ate like that and then didn’t feel guilty or ashamed. That’s really the hard part for me afterward.\n\nI mean, the reason I finally came in is because after those nights, when it’s over, I’ll be sitting there and there are crumbs on me or on the table, empty containers around, wrappers… and I just feel so ashamed. Disgusted with myself, really. It’s like the quiet comes back and I can see everything I did, and I think, “How did you let this happen again?”\n\nAnd then the next morning it follows me. I have trouble even praying sometimes because I feel so low about myself, and if I go to church I feel like I can hardly look people in the eye. I know they may not know anything, but I feel like it’s written all over me somehow.\n\nSo yes, I lose control while I’m eating, but afterward… no, I don’t feel fine about it. I feel terrible.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever eaten a large amount of food in a very short time and not felt guilty about it?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think so. Not the way you mean.\n\nWhen it’s one of those times where I’ve eaten a lot in a short stretch, afterward I don’t feel calm or like it was nothing. That’s actually a big part of why I came in. I’ll sort of come back to myself and be sitting there with crumbs on me, or on the chair, empty containers and wrappers around, and I just feel… ashamed. Really ashamed. Disgusted with myself, if I’m being honest.\n\nAnd it doesn’t just end when I clean it up. The next morning it’s still sitting on me. Sometimes I can hardly pray because I feel so low, and if I go to church I feel like I can’t look folks in the eye, even though they don’t know what happened. I just feel like they can somehow see it on me.\n\nSo no, I can’t say I’ve eaten like that and not felt guilty. The guilt is usually right there afterward.\n\nDoctor: Do you experience any physical symptoms, such as stomach pain or nausea, after these episodes of eating?\n\nPatient: Yes… I do. That part is very noticeable afterward.\n\nI’ll eat past the point where I know I should stop, and then I keep going anyway, and by the time it’s over my stomach feels tight, like stretched tight and sore. Sometimes I can feel it pressing under my nightgown, and it’s uncomfortable even just sitting there. It’s not just “oh, I’m full from supper” kind of full. It’s more like I’ve made myself so full that my body is complaining.\n\nAnd moving around afterward hurts. I’ve had times where I sort of have to ease myself into the recliner slowly, because if I bend too much or move too fast, my stomach feels sore and heavy. I’ll sit there trying to get comfortable, but there isn’t really a comfortable way to sit for a while. It’s embarrassing to say, but yes, I definitely get physical discomfort after.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever eaten a large amount of food in a short time without feeling guilty or ashamed about it afterward?\n\nPatient: No… not really. I know I’ve kind of said this, but that’s the part that stays with me the most. When I eat like that, afterward I don’t feel relieved or okay about it. I sit there and look around at the crumbs, the empty containers, the wrappers, and I just feel so ashamed of myself.\n\nIt’s not just a little guilt, either. I feel disgusted with myself, like I’ve done something I should’ve been able to stop. And the next morning it’s still there. Sometimes I can hardly pray because I feel so low, and if I go to church I feel like I can’t look people in the eye, even though they don’t know. I just feel like somehow it’s written all over me.\n\nSo no, I don’t think I’ve had those times without guilt or shame. The shame is usually right there afterward.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever eaten a large amount of food in a short time without feeling like you were in control while eating?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, that’s exactly what it feels like. Not every bite of food in my life, of course, but when it happens, it’s like once I start—especially after the house is quiet—something in me just… steps aside.\n\nI’ll take that first bite, and part of me already knows, Belinda, this is where it starts, you need to stop now. I can hear myself thinking it. I’m not confused about what I’m doing. But then my hands just keep reaching for more anyway, like my good sense is standing off in the corner and not in charge anymore.\n\nAnd I’ll tell myself, okay, that’s enough, put it away, just close the package, just get up and leave the room. But I don’t. I keep eating, even while I’m arguing with myself in my own head. That’s the part that scares me, honestly—the feeling that I’m watching myself do it and I can’t seem to make myself stop when I want to.\n\nDoctor: Is there ever a time when you feel like you've lost control while eating, but you don't feel guilty or ashamed about it afterward?\n\nPatient: No… I can’t really think of a time like that. If I’ve lost control with eating, the shame is right there afterward. That’s honestly one of the main reasons I came in, because after those nights I’ll just sit there and see the crumbs and the empty containers around me, and I feel so ashamed and disgusted with myself.\n\nIt’s not like I finish and just move on with my evening. I sit there thinking, Why did I do this again? Why couldn’t I stop? And then the next morning it follows me. I can hardly pray sometimes because I feel so low about myself, and if I go to church I feel like I can’t look folks in the eye, even though nobody there knows what happened in my kitchen the night before.\n\nSo no, if I feel out of control while I’m eating, I don’t come away from it feeling fine. I feel awful about it afterward.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
No… no, I really don’t think so. If I’ve gotten to that point where I feel like I’ve lost control, the guilt and shame come right behind it. That’s part of why I’m here, honestly.

After those nights, I’ll be sitting there with crumbs around me and empty containers, and it’s like I can’t even pretend it didn’t happen. I feel so ashamed of myself, and disgusted too, like I should have been able to stop and I didn’t. And it doesn’t just pass in a few minutes. The next morning I still feel it sitting on me.

Sometimes I can hardly pray because I feel so low, like I’m not even worthy to be talking to God after I’ve done that again. And if I go to church, I have trouble looking folks in the eye, even though nobody knows what happened. It feels like they could somehow see it on me.

So no… if I lose control with eating, I don’t come away feeling okay about it. I feel awful afterward.

I feel like I should have control, because I’m the one sitting there, I’m the one picking it up, you know? I can worry too much or put things off, and sometimes I feel like my thoughts run around in circles. But I don’t know if I’d call that losing control. Not like this. But what I’m coming in for—the thing that feels out of my hands—is really the eating.\n\nDoctor: Do you usually feel guilty after these episodes of eating?\n\nPatient: Yes… very guilty. Guilty almost isn’t a strong enough word sometimes. I’m a churchgoing woman, and I’ll try to pray, but sometimes I can hardly get the words out because I feel so ashamed and disgusted with myself. I’ll be around folks at church and not want to look them in the eye, even though they don’t know anything about it. It feels like they could somehow see it on me. So yes… afterward, the guilt is terrible.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had episodes of eating like that where you didn't feel guilty afterward?\n\nPatient: No… I can’t really think of a time where I didn’t feel guilty afterward. That’s a big part of why I came in, honestly. It’s not just the eating itself, though that’s bad enough. It’s what comes after.\n\nI’ll be sitting there afterward, and there’ll be crumbs on me, empty containers around me, and I just feel so ashamed and disgusted with myself. Like I’ve done something terrible, even if nobody else saw it. And the next morning it’s still with me. I’ll try to pray, and sometimes I can hardly even do that because I feel so low about myself.
